<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Central Nervous System Pathology: Fungal Infections
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the ability to differentiate between common fungal infections of the central nervous system (CNS), specifically focusing on the characteristic histopathology and clinical context of *Aspergillus* vs. *Mucor* infections, particularly in immunocompromised patients. (考察中枢神经系统真菌感染的鉴别，特别是 *曲霉菌* 和 *毛霉菌* 感染的组织病理学特征和临床背景，尤其是在免疫功能低下患者中。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with a history of poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and recent chemotherapy for acute myeloid leukemia presents with worsening headache, fever (38.5°C), and altered mental status over the past 3 days. He is neutropenic (ANC < 500/µL). A CT scan of the head reveals multiple ring-enhancing lesions with surrounding edema in the cerebrum and cerebellum. A stereotactic brain biopsy is performed. Microscopic examination of the brain tissue shows broad, irregularly shaped, non-septate hyphae branching at acute (90-degree) angles, infiltrating blood vessel walls and surrounding parenchyma. Gomori methenamine silver (GMS) stain highlights the fungal elements.

Which of the following is the most likely causative organism and underlying pathological mechanism?

(A) *Cryptococcus neoformans* causing gelatinous capsule formation and meningeal inflammation.
(B) *Aspergillus fumigatus* causing angioinvasive disease with septate hyphae branching at acute angles.
(C) *Mucorales* species causing angioinvasive disease with broad, non-septate hyphae branching at right angles.
(D) *Candida albicans* causing yeast forms and pseudohyphae invading the brain parenchyma.
(E) *Pneumocystis jirovecii* causing cystic lesions with intracellular organisms visible on GMS stain.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male, poorly controlled diabetes, recent chemotherapy for AML, neutropenic. This profile indicates significant immunosuppression, predisposing him to opportunistic infections, particularly fungal infections.
- **Symptoms:** Headache, fever, altered mental status. These are non-specific symptoms of CNS infection or inflammation.
- **Imaging:** CT scan shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions with edema. This pattern is suggestive of abscesses or infiltrative masses, common in fungal CNS infections.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Brain tissue shows broad, irregularly shaped, non-septate hyphae branching at acute (90-degree) angles, infiltrating blood vessel walls and surrounding parenchyma. GMS stain highlights the fungal elements. This is the crucial diagnostic clue.
    - **Broad, non-septate hyphae:** Rules out *Aspergillus* (septate) and *Candida* (yeast/pseudohyphae).
    - **Branching at acute (90-degree) angles:** This is the characteristic morphology of *Mucorales* species (e.g., *Rhizopus*, *Mucor*). *Aspergillus* typically branches at acute (45-degree) angles.
    - **Angioinvasion:** Infiltration of blood vessel walls is a hallmark of aggressive fungal infections like *Mucorales* and *Aspergillus*.
    - **GMS stain:** Confirms the presence of fungal elements.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- The patient's immunosuppression (neutropenia from chemotherapy, hyperglycemia from diabetes) creates an environment where opportunistic fungi can proliferate.
- *Mucorales* are ubiquitous molds that cause mucormycosis, an aggressive, often fatal angioinvasive fungal infection.
- The hyphae of *Mucorales* are broad (5-15 µm), irregularly shaped, and characteristically lack septations (cross-walls).
- The branching pattern is typically at right angles (90 degrees), unlike the dichotomous branching at 45-degree angles seen in *Aspergillus*.
- The angioinvasive nature of *Mucorales* leads to thrombosis, infarction, and necrosis of the infected tissue, explaining the ring-enhancing lesions with edema seen on imaging.
- The mechanism involves the fungus invading blood vessels, causing endothelial damage, thrombosis, and subsequent tissue ischemia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) *Cryptococcus neoformans*: This yeast causes cryptococcosis, often presenting as meningitis in immunocompromised patients. Histologically, it is characterized by encapsulated yeast cells (appearing as clear halos around the organisms on India ink stain or H&E) and often causes gelatinous meningitis. The biopsy description (broad, non-septate hyphae) is inconsistent with *Cryptococcus*.
- (B) *Aspergillus fumigatus*: This mold causes aspergillosis. While it also causes angioinvasive disease, especially in neutropenic patients, its hyphae are septate (have cross-walls) and typically branch at acute angles (around 45 degrees), not the 90-degree angles described in the vignette.
- (D) *Candida albicans*: This yeast causes candidiasis. CNS involvement is less common than *Cryptococcus* or *Mucorales*. Histologically, it typically shows yeast forms and pseudohyphae (chains of elongated budding yeast cells). True hyphae are rare. The biopsy description is inconsistent with *Candida*.
- (E) *Pneumocystis jirovecii*: This fungus causes *Pneumocystis* pneumonia (PCP), primarily affecting the lungs. CNS involvement is rare. Histologically, it appears as small, cup-shaped trophozoites or cysts within alveolar spaces (in the lung) or sometimes brain tissue. GMS stain highlights the cysts, but the morphology is distinct from the broad hyphae described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature          | *Mucorales* (Correct) | *Aspergillus* | *Cryptococcus* | *Candida* | *Pneumocystis* |
|-------------------|-----------------------|---------------|----------------|-----------|----------------|
| **Morphology**    | Broad, non-septate hyphae | Septate hyphae | Encapsulated yeast | Yeast/Pseudohyphae | Trophozoites/Cysts |
| **Branching Angle** | ~90 degrees           | ~45 degrees   | N/A            | N/A       | N/A            |
| **Angioinvasion** | Yes                   | Yes           | No             | Rare      | No             |
| **Typical Site**  | Sinuses, lungs, brain  | Lungs, brain  | Meninges, lungs | Mucosa, blood | Lungs           |
| **Patient Risk**  | Diabetes, iron overload, neutropenia | Neutropenia, transplant | HIV, steroids | Immunosuppression | HIV, immunosuppression |
| **Stain**         | GMS, PAS              | GMS, PAS      | India Ink, GMS | GMS, PAS  | GMS, PAS       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key to differentiating CNS fungal infections on biopsy lies in the morphology of the fungal elements. *Mucorales* are characterized by broad, non-septate hyphae branching at right angles, often causing angioinvasive disease in immunocompromised patients. *Aspergillus* has septate hyphae branching at acute angles. *Cryptococcus* presents as encapsulated yeast. *Candida* shows yeast and pseudohyphae. *Pneumocystis* is primarily a lung pathogen with distinct cyst forms. Remember the classic branching angle difference: 90 degrees for *Mucorales*, 45 degrees for *Aspergillus*.
